People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

It just feels a bit out of proportion to what’s actually happening, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped doing anything important to you because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Not stopped, exactly… I mean, I’m still working and I’m still trying to keep up with things. But I have noticed some work tasks take me much longer than they used to. Even a straightforward listing negotiation or reviewing a contract, I’ll go over it again and again because I’m worried I missed something.\n\nAnd sometimes I delay returning calls because I don’t quite feel confident that I’ve checked everything carefully enough yet. I know that probably sounds overly cautious, but I don’t want to make a mistake. Does that count as avoiding something?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s ruining things, exactly… I’m still managing. But yes, I suppose it is interfering some. At work, even simple listing negotiations or contract reviews are taking me a lot longer than they used to because I keep checking myself. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

I’m not sure I’d call it restless exactly… maybe a little keyed up sometimes. When I’m worrying, I don’t always feel settled, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it.

I mean, I do get worked up and uneasy, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had an episode like that—unless I’m misunderstanding what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Avoiding… maybe not exactly avoiding, no. I still go to work and do what needs doing. But I suppose I do put things off a bit more than I used to. Even simple listing negotiations or reviewing contracts take me longer now, because I keep feeling like I should check everything one more time. And I’ve delayed returning some calls until I feel more certain I haven’t missed something. It’s not terrible, I don’t think, but… it is slowing me down. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms like headaches, tremors, or stomach issues when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I wouldn’t want to make too much of it, but by the end of the day my neck and shoulders can feel really tight, like I’ve been sort of bracing myself without realizing it. And sometimes when I’m driving between listings or sitting at my desk, I notice I’m clenching my jaw. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of physical thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I have my moments where I feel worn out or a little discouraged, but I wouldn’t say I’m sad or down most of the time. It’s more like… I get tense or preoccupied, if that makes sense. But not depressed, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring thoughts that feel intrusive or repetitive?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, yes. I’m not sure I’d call them intrusive exactly, but I do get stuck on the same concern. Is that what you mean by repetitive thoughts?\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mostly about one area of your life or are they spread across many different things?\n\nPatient: It’s… probably spread out, honestly. I wish I could say it was just work, because that would make sense with the market and clients and all that, but it’s not only that. I worry about my family, money, whether moving to the condo was really the right decision… little things that probably don’t deserve as much attention as I give them. It just feels a bit out of proportion to what’s actually happening, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped doing anything important to you because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Not stopped, exactly… I mean, I’m still working and I’m still trying to keep up with things. Before I know it, I’ve spent half an hour replaying small details that I really meant to move past. It just seems a bit out of proportion to what’s actually going on, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that sudden, overwhelming way where it peaks quickly. I mean, I do get worked up and uneasy, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had an episode like that—unless I’m misunderstanding what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Avoiding… maybe not exactly avoiding, no. I still go to work and do what needs doing. But I suppose I do put things off a bit more than I used to. Even simple listing negotiations or reviewing contracts take me longer now, because I keep feeling like I should check everything one more time. And I’ve delayed returning some calls until I feel more certain I haven’t missed something. It’s not terrible, I don’t think, but… it is slowing me down. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms like headaches, tremors, or stomach issues when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I wouldn’t want to make too much of it, but by the end of the day my neck and shoulders can feel really tight, like I’ve been sort of bracing myself without realizing it. And sometimes when I’m driving between listings or sitting at my desk, I notice I’m clenching my jaw. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of physical thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I have my moments where I feel worn out or a little discouraged, but I wouldn’t say I’m sad or down most of the time. It’s more like… I get tense or preoccupied, if that makes sense. But not depressed, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring thoughts that feel intrusive or repetitive?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, yes. I’m not sure I’d call them intrusive exactly, but I do get stuck on the same concern. Even when I tell myself, “Parin, this is not urgent,” one thing leads to another, and before I know it I’ve spent half an hour replaying small details I meant to just let go. Is that what you mean by repetitive thoughts?\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mostly about one area of your life or are they spread across many different things?\n\nPatient: It’s… probably spread out, honestly. I wish I could say it was just work, because that would make sense with the market and clients and all that, but it’s not only that. I worry about my family, money, whether moving to the condo was really the right decision… little things that probably don’t deserve as much attention as I give them. It just feels a bit out of proportion to what’s actually happening, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped doing anything important to you because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Not stopped, exactly… I mean, I’m still working and I’m still trying to keep up with things. But I have noticed some work tasks take me much longer than they used to. Even a straightforward listing negotiation or reviewing a contract, I’ll go over it again and again because I’m worried I missed something.\n\nAnd sometimes I delay returning calls because I don’t quite feel confident that I’ve checked everything carefully enough yet. I know that probably sounds overly cautious, but I don’t want to make a mistake. Does that count as avoiding something?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s ruining things, exactly… I’m still managing. But yes, I suppose it is interfering some. At work, even simple listing negotiations or contract reviews are taking me a lot longer than they used to because I keep checking myself. And I’ve delayed returning calls because I don’t feel quite confident that I’ve looked everything over carefully enough.\n\nI know that may sound a bit excessive, but I’m trying to be responsible, not difficult. Still… it does get in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, though I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. I came in because I seem to worry about everything now—clients, the market, my family, money, even whether moving to the condo was the right decision.\n\nAnd I know everyone worries about those things, but mine feels… out of proportion to what’s actually happening. So yes, it is distressing, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to repeat certain actions or check things over and over as part of your worrying?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t think of it as a ritual or anything like that. It’s more that at work, even things I used to handle pretty smoothly—like a listing negotiation or reviewing a contract—are taking me much longer now because I keep going back over them.\n\nAnd then sometimes I put off returning calls because I don’t feel quite sure I’ve checked everything carefully enough. I know that may sound like I’m being overly cautious… I’m just trying not to miss anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive thoughts are unwanted and difficult to control?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds like I’m not in control, and I do try to be reasonable with myself.\n\nI’ll even tell myself, “Parin, this is not urgent, you can let this go,” but then one concern sort of leads into another. Before I know it, I’ve spent half an hour replaying small details that I really meant to move past. It’s not constant every minute, but when it starts, it can be hard to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it restless exactly… maybe a little keyed up sometimes. When I’m worrying, I don’t always feel settled, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like I have a hard time just relaxing into whatever I’m doing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel muscle tension when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes… somewhat. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but by the end of the day my neck and shoulders are often pretty tight, like I’ve been bracing myself without realizing it.\n\nAnd I do sometimes catch myself clenching my jaw, especially when I’m driving between listings or sitting at my desk. I suppose that could be related to the worrying, couldn’t it?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Even a straightforward listing negotiation or reviewing a contract, I’ll go over it again and again because I’m worried I missed something.\n\nAnd sometimes I delay returning calls because I don’t quite feel confident that I’ve checked everything carefully enough yet. I know that probably sounds overly cautious, but I don’t want to make a mistake. Does that count as avoiding something?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s ruining things, exactly… I’m still managing. But yes, I suppose it is interfering some. At work, even simple listing negotiations or contract reviews are taking me a lot longer than they used to because I keep checking myself. And I’ve delayed returning calls because I don’t feel quite confident that I’ve looked everything over carefully enough.\n\nI know that may sound a bit excessive, but I’m trying to be responsible, not difficult. Still… it does get in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, though I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. I came in because I seem to worry about everything now—clients, the market, my family, money, even whether moving to the condo was the right decision.\n\nAnd I know everyone worries about those things, but mine feels… out of proportion to what’s actually happening. So yes, it is distressing, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to repeat certain actions or check things over and over as part of your worrying?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t think of it as a ritual or anything like that. It’s more that at work, even things I used to handle pretty smoothly—like a listing negotiation or reviewing a contract—are taking me much longer now because I keep going back over them.\n\nAnd then sometimes I put off returning calls because I don’t feel quite sure I’ve checked everything carefully enough. I know that may sound like I’m being overly cautious… I’m just trying not to miss anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive thoughts are unwanted and difficult to control?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds like I’m not in control, and I do try to be reasonable with myself.\n\nI’ll even tell myself, “Parin, this is not urgent, you can let this go,” but then one concern sort of leads into another. Before I know it, I’ve spent half an hour replaying small details that I really meant to move past. It’s not constant every minute, but when it starts, it can be hard to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it restless exactly… maybe a little keyed up sometimes. When I’m worrying, I don’t always feel settled, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like I have a hard time just relaxing into whatever I’m doing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel muscle tension when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes… somewhat. I suppose that could be related to the worrying, couldn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind even when you try to think of something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose they do. I try not to indulge them, if that makes sense. I’ll tell myself, “Parin, this is not urgent, you don’t need to keep going over it,” but then one worry sort of connects to another.\n\nAnd then I realize I’ve spent, I don’t know, half an hour replaying little details I meant to let go of. Before I know it, I’ve spent half an hour replaying small details that I really meant to move past. Still… it does get in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, though I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. I came in because I seem to worry about everything now—clients, the market, my family, money, even whether moving to the condo was the right decision.\n\nAnd I know everyone worries about those things, but mine feels… out of proportion to what’s actually happening. So yes, it is distressing, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to repeat certain actions or check things over and over as part of your worrying?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t think of it as a ritual or anything like that. It’s more that at work, even things I used to handle pretty smoothly—like a listing negotiation or reviewing a contract—are taking me much longer now because I keep going back over them.\n\nAnd then sometimes I put off returning calls because I don’t feel quite sure I’ve checked everything carefully enough. I know that may sound like I’m being overly cautious… I’m just trying not to miss anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive thoughts are unwanted and difficult to control?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds like I’m not in control, and I do try to be reasonable with myself.\n\nI’ll even tell myself, “Parin, this is not urgent, you can let this go,” but then one concern sort of leads into another. Before I know it, I’ve spent half an hour replaying small details that I really meant to move past. It’s not constant every minute, but when it starts, it can be hard to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it restless exactly… maybe a little keyed up sometimes. When I’m worrying, I don’t always feel settled, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like I have a hard time just relaxing into whatever I’m doing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel muscle tension when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes… somewhat. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but by the end of the day my neck and shoulders are often pretty tight, like I’ve been bracing myself without realizing it.\n\nAnd I do sometimes catch myself clenching my jaw, especially when I’m driving between listings or sitting at my desk. I suppose that could be related to the worrying, couldn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind even when you try to think of something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose they do. I try not to indulge them, if that makes sense. I’ll tell myself, “Parin, this is not urgent, you don’t need to keep going over it,” but then one worry sort of connects to another.\n\nAnd then I realize I’ve spent, I don’t know, half an hour replaying little details I meant to let go of. It’s not because I want to be thinking about them. It just takes more effort to redirect than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your worrying affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any major way. I mean, I try pretty hard not to put my worries onto other people, especially my family.\n\nMaybe I’m a little distracted sometimes, or I might ask for reassurance more than I should, but I wouldn’t say it’s causing real problems with relationships. At least, I hope not. Does that sound like what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your worrying is significantly interfering with your ability to perform your job or maintain your social relationships?\n\nPatient: Maybe not significantly in the sense that I’m failing at my job or anything… I’m still getting things done. But yes, it has slowed me down more than I like to admit.\n\nEven straightforward listing negotiations or reviewing contracts can take me much longer than they used to, because I keep feeling like I need to check everything one more time. And I have delayed returning calls now and then because I don’t feel confident I’ve looked things over carefully enough yet.\n\nSocially, I think I’m mostly managing, but with work… yes, there is some interference. Does that count as significant?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is making it hard for you to enjoy time with friends and family or causing problems in your relationships with them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s causing problems, no… at least not that anyone has said to me. Even simple listing negotiations or reviewing contracts take me longer now, because I keep feeling like I should check everything one more time. And I’ve delayed returning some calls until I feel more certain I haven’t missed something. It’s not terrible, I don’t think, but… it is slowing me down. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms like headaches, tremors, or stomach issues when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I wouldn’t want to make too much of it, but by the end of the day my neck and shoulders can feel really tight, like I’ve been sort of bracing myself without realizing it. And sometimes when I’m driving between listings or sitting at my desk, I notice I’m clenching my jaw. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of physical thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I have my moments where I feel worn out or a little discouraged, but I wouldn’t say I’m sad or down most of the time. It’s more like… I get tense or preoccupied, if that makes sense. But not depressed, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring thoughts that feel intrusive or repetitive?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, yes. I’m not sure I’d call them intrusive exactly, but I do get stuck on the same concern. Even when I tell myself, “Parin, this is not urgent,” one thing leads to another, and before I know it I’ve spent half an hour replaying small details I meant to just let go. Is that what you mean by repetitive thoughts?\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mostly about one area of your life or are they spread across many different things?\n\nPatient: It’s… probably spread out, honestly. I wish I could say it was just work, because that would make sense with the market and clients and all that, but it’s not only that. I worry about my family, money, whether moving to the condo was really the right decision… little things that probably don’t deserve as much attention as I give them. It just feels a bit out of proportion to what’s actually happening, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped doing anything important to you because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Not stopped, exactly… I mean, I’m still working and I’m still trying to keep up with things. But I have noticed some work tasks take me much longer than they used to. Even a straightforward listing negotiation or reviewing a contract, I’ll go over it again and again because I’m worried I missed something.\n\nAnd sometimes I delay returning calls because I don’t quite feel confident that I’ve checked everything carefully enough yet. I know that probably sounds overly cautious, but I don’t want to make a mistake. Does that count as avoiding something?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s ruining things, exactly… I’m still managing. But yes, I suppose it is interfering some. At work, even simple listing negotiations or contract reviews are taking me a lot longer than they used to because I keep checking myself. And I’ve delayed returning calls because I don’t feel quite confident that I’ve looked everything over carefully enough.\n\nI know that may sound a bit excessive, but I’m trying to be responsible, not difficult. Still… it does get in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, though I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. I came in because I seem to worry about everything now—clients, the market, my family, money, even whether moving to the condo was the right decision.\n\nAnd I know everyone worries about those things, but mine feels… out of proportion to what’s actually happening. So yes, it is distressing, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to repeat certain actions or check things over and over as part of your worrying?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t think of it as a ritual or anything like that. It’s more that at work, even things I used to handle pretty smoothly—like a listing negotiation or reviewing a contract—are taking me much longer now because I keep going back over them.\n\nAnd then sometimes I put off returning calls because I don’t feel quite sure I’ve checked everything carefully enough. I know that may sound like I’m being overly cautious… I’m just trying not to miss anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive thoughts are unwanted and difficult to control?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds like I’m not in control, and I do try to be reasonable with myself.\n\nI’ll even tell myself, “Parin, this is not urgent, you can let this go,” but then one concern sort of leads into another. Before I know it, I’ve spent half an hour replaying small details that I really meant to move past. It’s not constant every minute, but when it starts, it can be hard to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it restless exactly… maybe a little keyed up sometimes. When I’m worrying, I don’t always feel settled, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like I have a hard time just relaxing into whatever I’m doing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel muscle tension when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes… somewhat. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but by the end of the day my neck and shoulders are often pretty tight, like I’ve been bracing myself without realizing it.\n\nAnd I do sometimes catch myself clenching my jaw, especially when I’m driving between listings or sitting at my desk. I suppose that could be related to the worrying, couldn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind even when you try to think of something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose they do. I try not to indulge them, if that makes sense. I’ll tell myself, “Parin, this is not urgent, you don’t need to keep going over it,” but then one worry sort of connects to another.\n\nAnd then I realize I’ve spent, I don’t know, half an hour replaying little details I meant to let go of. It’s not because I want to be thinking about them. It just takes more effort to redirect than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your worrying affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any major way. I mean, I try pretty hard not to put my worries onto other people, especially my family.\n\nMaybe I’m a little distracted sometimes, or I might ask for reassurance more than I should, but I wouldn’t say it’s causing real problems with relationships. At least, I hope not. Does that sound like what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your worrying is significantly interfering with your ability to perform your job or maintain your social relationships?\n\nPatient: Maybe not significantly in the sense that I’m failing at my job or anything… I’m still getting things done. But yes, it has slowed me down more than I like to admit.\n\nEven straightforward listing negotiations or reviewing contracts can take me much longer than they used to, because I keep feeling like I need to check everything one more time. And I have delayed returning calls now and then because I don’t feel confident I’ve looked things over carefully enough yet.\n\nSocially, I think I’m mostly managing, but with work… yes, there is some interference. Does that count as significant?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is making it hard for you to enjoy time with friends and family or causing problems in your relationships with them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s causing problems, no… at least not that anyone has said to me. I’m still spending time with people, and I try to be present.\n\nMaybe sometimes I’m a bit in my head, or I don’t relax as much as I’d like, but I wouldn’t say it’s damaging my relationships. I hope that’s an honest enough answer.\n\nDoctor: Is there any area of your life where you feel you don't worry at all?\n\nPatient: Honestly… I’m trying to think of one, and I’m not sure there is. That sounds dramatic, and I don’t mean it that way, but lately it feels like my mind can find something to worry about in almost every area.\n\nClients, the market, my family, money… even whether moving to the condo was the right decision. Some of it is normal, I know, but it feels bigger than what’s actually happening. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is there a specific fear or concern that you find yourself worrying about the most?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say there was just one specific thing, but it seems more spread out than that. Work is probably the easiest one to point to—clients and the market, whether I’m making the right call, things like that.\n\nBut then it turns into family, money, even whether moving to the condo was the right decision. I know some of those are normal things to think about, but lately it feels like I worry about all of them more than the situation really calls for. Does that answer what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
